Therefore, our upper bound is only asymptotically tight for instances such that
T_{\mathrm{KL}}^{\star}(\bm{\nu})=\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{TV}}^{\star}(\bm{\nu})/\Delta_{\max})
, e.g. instances where the mean gaps have the same order of magnitude.
In all the other cases, the plug-in approach is sub-optimal due to a problem-dependent gap.

The major limitation of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
lies in the fact that the transportation costs are independent of the privacy budget.
Without accounting for the knowledge that there is an additional Laplace “noise”, there is little hope to match the asymptotic lower bound in the high-privacy regime. We remedy this issue with
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
in Section
4.4
.

Non-asymptotic confidence regime for
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
.

Studying
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
for any confidence level requires to adapt the proof of
Jourdan and Degenne (2024)
.
The
\delta
-dependent term in their upper bound is worse than for an asymptotic analysis (i.e., Theorem
22
).
Therefore, we focus on their dominating
\delta
-independent term that stems from
Jourdan and Degenne (2024, Lemma 3.2)
, i.e.,
B_{n}=a^{\star}
except for a sublinear number of times.
While the intution behind their proof still holds, using the
DAF
(\epsilon)
estimator adds steps to cope for the Laplace “noise”, doubling and forgetting.
If
B_{n}=a
with
a\neq a^{\star}
and empirical means do not deviate too much from the true means, then we have
N_{n,a}=\mathcal{O}(\log(n)\Delta_{\epsilon,a}^{-2})
where
\Delta_{\epsilon,a}\mathrel{\triangleq}\epsilon(\sqrt{1+2\Delta_{a}/\epsilon}-1)
by solving for
N_{n,a}/\log n
the quadratic inequality

\mu_{a^{\star}}\lesssim\tilde{\mu}_{n,a^{\star}}+b_{a^{\star}}^{G,\epsilon}(\tilde{N}_{n})\leq\tilde{\mu}_{n,a}+b_{a}^{G,\epsilon}(\tilde{N}_{n})\lesssim\mu_{a}+2b_{a}^{G,\epsilon}(\tilde{N}_{n})\leq\mu_{a}+2\left(\sqrt{\frac{4\log n}{N_{n,a}}}+\frac{4\log n}{\epsilon N_{n,a}}\right)\>,

where
\tilde{N}_{n,a}\geq N_{n,a}/4
due to doubling and forgetting, and
\lesssim
stems from the “heuristic” choice of
(b^{G,\epsilon}_{a})_{a\in[K]}
, which do not yield valid upper confidence bounds.
Since
N_{n,a}
is bounded and incremented by one half of the time (tracking for
\beta=1/2
), the event
B_{n}\neq a^{\star}
occurs less than
\mathcal{O}(H(\bm{\nu},\epsilon)\log n)
times, where
H(\bm{\nu},\epsilon)=\sum_{a\in[K]}\Delta_{\epsilon,a}^{-2}
and
\Delta_{\epsilon,a^{\star}}=\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}\Delta_{\epsilon,a}
.
The rest of the proof can be adapted similarly, with extra terms due to the Laplace “noise”, doubling and forgetting.
Intuitively, it holds thanks to the use of the Gaussian transportation costs
W^{G}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
3
) providing time-uniform separability between the means and the allocations, i.e., ratio of the squared mean gap and the sum of the inverse allocation.
In summary, we conjecture that
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
satisfies a non-asymptotic upper bound whose dominating
\delta
-independent term scale as
\mathcal{O}\left(\left(H(\bm{\nu},\epsilon)\log H(\bm{\nu},\epsilon)\right)^{\alpha}\right)
for some
\alpha>1
, where
\alpha
would be an algorithmic hyperparameter involved in a valid choice for
(b^{G,\epsilon}_{a})_{a\in[K]}
.
As
\Delta_{\epsilon,a}^{2}/\epsilon\to_{\epsilon\to 0}\Delta_{a}
and
\Delta_{\epsilon,a}^{2}\to_{\epsilon\to+\infty}\Delta_{a}^{2}
, the bound suffers from a suboptimal dependency due to the power
\alpha
.

4.4
A
Modified Transportation Cost
Approach: the
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
Algorithm

To overcome the limitation of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
, one should adapt the transportation costs to reflect the lower bound (Theorem
17
) instead of “ignoring” the privacy constraint by using the transportation costs
W^{G}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
3
) which are tailored for non-private FC-BAI.

\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
algorithm.

We propose the
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
algorithm
, whose standalone pseudocode is detailed in Algorithm
10
(Appendix
C
).

It differs from
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
solely by (i) the use of a modified transportation costs in the GLR stopping rule and the TC challenger, and (ii) an adequate stopping threshold to ensure
\delta
-correctness.

\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
is an instance of Algorithm
1
using the
DAF
(\epsilon)
estimator (Algorithm
5
),

W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}(\tilde{\mu},\omega)=\frac{(\tilde{\mu}_{a}-\tilde{\mu}_{b})_{+}\min\{\epsilon/2,(\tilde{\mu}_{a}-\tilde{\mu}_{b})_{+}\}}{2\sigma^{2}(1/\omega_{a}+1/\omega_{b})}\quad\text{with}\quad\sigma=1/2\>,

(13)

b^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
11
),
and
\tilde{c}^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}(\tilde{\mu},\omega,\delta)
as in Eq. (
14
), which yields
\delta
-correctness for any sampling rule (Lemma
23
).

Lemma 23
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\overline{W}_{-1}(x)=-W_{-1}(-e^{-x})
for all
x\geq 1
, where
W_{-1}
is the negative branch of the Lambert
W
function.
It satisfies
\overline{W}_{-1}(x)\approx x+\log x
.
Let
c^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
12
),
k(x)=\log_{2}(x)+2
and

h(x,\delta)=\overline{W}_{-1}\left(2\log(\pi^{2}Kk(x)^{2}/(2\delta))+4\log(4+\log x)+1/2\right)/2\>.

Given any sampling rule, combining the
DAF
(\epsilon)
estimator with the GLR stopping rule as in Eq. (
2
) with
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
13
) and the stopping threshold
\tilde{c}^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}(\tilde{\mu},\omega,\delta)
which is equal to

\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}c^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}(\omega,2\delta/3)+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\epsilon\sigma}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\sqrt{\frac{h(\omega_{c},\delta)}{\omega_{c}}}\log\left(\frac{\pi^{2}Kk(\omega_{c})^{2}}{2\delta}\right)&\text{if }(\tilde{\mu}_{a}-\tilde{\mu}_{b})_{+}<\epsilon/2\\
\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\log\left(\pi^{2}K\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}k(w_{c})/(2\delta)\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{2}\sigma}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\sqrt{\omega_{c}h(\omega_{c},\delta)}&\end{cases}\>,

(14)

yields a
\delta
-correct algorithm for any
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian distributions with unique best arm.

Our threshold is
\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\log(1/\delta)+\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{2}\sigma}(\sqrt{\omega_{b}}+\sqrt{\omega_{a}})\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
when
\tilde{\mu}_{a}-\tilde{\mu}_{b}\geq\epsilon/2
, and

\log(1/\delta)+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}(1/\omega_{a}+1/\omega_{b})\log(1/\delta)^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\epsilon\sigma}(\sqrt{1/\omega_{a}}+\sqrt{1/\omega_{b}})\log(1/\delta)^{3/2}\quad\text{otherwise.}

While the proof bares ressemblence to the one of Lemma
21
(see Appendix
F.3
), the main novelty in
\tilde{c}^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
is that it depends on whether
(\tilde{\mu}_{n,a}-\tilde{\mu}_{n,b})_{+}
lies above
\epsilon/2
or below it.
This is instrumental to switch between the high-privacy regime, where the Laplace “noise” dominates, and the low-privacy regime, where the Laplace “noise” is “negligible”
In the latter case, we recover
c^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
, with an improved factor
1/2
due to tighter concentration inequalities.
In the former case, the dominating term in the stopping threshold stems from the control of the Laplace “noise”, while the Gaussian “noise” contributes to second-order terms.
To the best of our knownledge, Lemma
23
constitutes the first mean-aware stopping thresholds.
This idea might be of independent interest, especially when several sources of randomness coexist in the GLR stopping rule.
Echoing the discussion in Section
4.1
, once a measure of “distinguishability” at the arm’s level is obtained, we conjecture that controlling its empirical version in a mean-agnostic fashion is possible.

The transportation cost
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
is inspired by
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})
as in Eq. (
10
).
Recall that the associated
\beta
-characteristic time
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})
in Eq. (
5
) is defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})}^{-1}\mathrel{\triangleq}\max\limits_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K},\omega_{a^{\star}}=\beta}\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}\frac{(\mu_{a^{\star}}-\mu_{a})\min\{\epsilon/2,\mu_{a^{\star}}-\mu_{a}\}}{2\sigma^{2}(1/\beta+1/\omega_{a})}\quad\text{with}\quad\sigma=1/2\>.

(15)

The algorithmic intuition behind
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
is the same as for
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
.
The main difference lies in the behavior of the TC challenger that now aims at reaching the equality for all the empirical modified transporation costs
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a^{\star},a}
compared to
W^{G}_{a^{\star},a}
for
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
.
Given that
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
is designed to reach
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})
asymptotically, we will not achieve our lower bound, as
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})
is only an upper bound on
T^{\star}_{g}(\bm{\nu};\epsilon)
(Corollary
18
).

Asymptotic upper bound
.
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
achieves an asymptotic upper bound on its expected sample complexity as
T_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}^{\star}
evaluated at
\bm{\nu}
in the low privacy regime and at
\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon}
in the high-privacy regime, up to multiplicative privacy-aware cost (Theorem
24
).

Theorem 24
.

Let
(\delta,\beta)\in(0,1)^{2}
and
\epsilon>0
.
The
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
algorithm is
\epsilon
-global DP,
\delta
-correct and satisfies that, for any Bernoulli
\bm{\nu}
instance with distinct means
\mu\in(0,1)^{K}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\nu}}\left[\tau_{\delta}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}4T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu})g_{1}\left(\Delta_{\max}/(\sigma^{2}\epsilon)\right)&\text{if }\Delta_{\max}<\epsilon/2\\
2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})}g_{2}(\epsilon^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})}\max\{\beta,1-\beta\}/4)/\sigma^{2}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\>,

where
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}
as in Eq. (
5
) for
\sigma=1/2
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})}
as in Eq. (
15
).
The function
g_{1}(y)=\sup\left\{x\mid x^{2}<x+y\sqrt{2x}+\frac{y^{2}}{4}\right\}
is increasing on
[0,12]
and satisfies that
g_{1}(0)=1
and
g_{1}(12)\leq 10
.
The function
g_{2}(y)=1+2(\sqrt{1+1/y}-1)^{-1}
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+}
and satisfies that
\lim_{y\to 0}g_{2}(y)=1
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the one of Theorem
22
, hence we defer to Appendix
G
for more details.
While
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
is used instead of
W^{G}_{a,b}
, both arguments rely on similar properties holding for Gaussian-like transportation costs.
The main technical novelty occurs during the “inversion” of the GLR stopping rule since the stopping threshold is mean-aware and includes additional second order terms (Appendix
G.5
).

∎

Discussion.

When
\Delta_{\max}<\epsilon/2
, our upper bound recovers the non-private lower bound for Gaussian distributions
T_{\mathrm{KL}}^{\star}(\bm{\nu})
up to a multiplicative factor
8g_{1}(4\Delta_{\max}/\epsilon)\in[8,80]
, whose limit is
8
in non-private regime where
\epsilon\to+\infty
.
When
\Delta_{\min}\geq\epsilon/2
, we have
2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL},\beta}(\bm{\nu}_{G,\epsilon})}\leq 4T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})/\epsilon
.
In the asymptotic highly privacy regime where
\epsilon\to 0
, our upper bound matches the lower bound up to a multiplicative factor
16
.
Therefore, we close the gap left open by the algorithm in Section
4.3
.
While the regime
\Delta_{\max}\geq\epsilon/2>\Delta_{\min}
is relevant for practical application, it is harder to understand how the different quantities interact in the upper/lower bounds in transitional phases.
Thus,
we do not
claim optimality in those phases.
Having matching upper and lower bounds
only
for high privacy regimes is an interesting phenomenon that appears in different settings of differential privacy literature, such as regret minimisation
(
Azize and Basu, 2022
)
, parameter estimation
(
Cai et al., 2021
)
and hidden probabilistic graphical models
(
Nikolakakis et al., 2019
)
.

For regret minimisation and BAI, we conjecture that deriving matching bounds at any privacy level fundamentally requires a measure of “distinguishability” at the arm’s level. This is a promising research direction that would require finer technical tools to optimally merge the privacy and the correctness constraints.

Comparison to DP-SE.

DP-SE
(
Sajed and Sheffet, 2019
)
is an
\epsilon
-global DP version of the Successive Elimination algorithm introduced for the regret minimisation setting.
The algorithm samples active arms uniformly during phases of geometrically increasing length.
Based on the private confidence bounds, DP-SE eliminates provably sub-optimal arms at the end of each phase.
Due to its phased-elimination structure, DP-SE can be easily converted into an
\epsilon
-global DP FC-BAI algorithm, where we stop once there is only one active arm left.
In particular, the proof of Theorem 4.3 of
Sajed and Sheffet (2019)
shows that with high probability any sub-optimal arm
a\neq a^{\star}
is sampled no more than
\text{O}(\Delta_{a}^{2}+(\epsilon\Delta_{a})^{-1})
.
From this result, it is straightforward to extract a sample complexity upper bound for DP-SE, i.e.
\text{O}(\sum_{a\neq a^{\star}}\Delta_{a}^{-2}+\sum_{a\neq a^{\star}}(\epsilon\Delta_{a})^{-1}).
This shows that DP-SE too achieves (ignoring constants) the high-privacy lower bound
T^{\star}_{\textrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})/\epsilon
for Bernoulli instances.
However, due to its uniform sampling within the phases, DP-SE is less adaptive than
TTUCB
.
Inside a phase, DP-SE continues to sample arms that might already be known to be bad, while
TTUCB
adapts its sampling rule based on the transportation costs that reflect the amount of evidence collected in favour of the hypothesis that the leader is the best arm.
Finally,
TTUCB
has the advantage of being anytime, i.e. its sampling strategy does not depend on the risk
\delta
.

Another adaptation of DP-SE, namely DP-SEQ, is proposed in
Kalogerias et al. (2021)
for the problem of privately finding the arm with the highest quantile at a fixed level, hence it is different from BAI.
For multiple agents,
Rio et al. (2023)
studies privacy for BAI under fixed confidence.
They propose and analyse the sample complexity of DP-MASE, a multi-agent version of DP-SE.
They show that multi-agent collaboration leads to better sample complexity than independent agents, even under privacy constraints.
While the multi-agent setting with federated learning allows tackling large-scale clinical trials taking place at several locations simultaneously, we study the single-agent setting, which is relevant for many small-scale clinical trials (see Example
1
).

Non-asymptotic confidence regime for
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
.

\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
has the same property on the leader as
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
, i.e.,
B_{n}=a^{\star}
except for a sublinear number of times.
However, the modified transportation costs
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
13
) do not provide time-uniform separability between the means and the allocations.
As it depends on
\min\{\epsilon/2,(\tilde{\mu}_{a}-\tilde{\mu}_{b})_{+}\}
in the numerator, we should justifty that
\tilde{\mu}_{n,a^{\star}}-\tilde{\mu}_{n,C_{n}}
and
\mu_{a^{\star}}-\mu_{C_{n}}
lies on the same side as
\epsilon/2
, in order to use the equality at equilibrium of the modified transportation costs to obtain
T^{\star}_{\epsilon,\beta}(\bm{\nu})^{-1}
.
It is challenging to prove this property non-asymptotically without incurring a larger
\delta
-independent dependency.
Therefore, we conjecture that
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
suffers from a worse non-asymptotic upper bound based on the proof of
Jourdan and Degenne (2024)
.
While our asymptotic analysis deals with this subtelty, the extra cost vanishes as
\delta\to 0
.

Remark 25

(On the number of rounds of adaptivity)
.

Used on any existing FC-BAI algorithm, the
DAF
update yields a batched algorithm, which satisfies
\epsilon
-global DP.
At the end of the episode of arm
a
(after updating its mean), it is possible to compute the sequence of all the arms to be pulled before the end of the next episode (for another arm), without taking the collected observations into account.
In contrast to the classical batched setting where the batch size is fixed, the size of the resulting batches is adaptive and data-dependent.
In the non-private setting (
\epsilon=+\infty
), we recover Batched Best-Arm Identification (BBAI) in the fixed-confidence setting.

\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
are asymptotically optimal up to a multiplicative factor
4
with solely
\mathcal{O}\left(K\log_{2}(T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu})\log(1/\delta))\right)
rounds of adaptivity.
We refer the reader to Appendix
H
for more details, including comparison to existing works.

5
Experimental Analysis

We perform experiments for both
\epsilon
-local DP and
\epsilon
-global DP. The code is available at
https://github.com/achraf-azize/DP-BAI
.

5.1
Local DP

We run
\mathsf{CTB\text{-}TT}
in different Bernoulli instances as in
Sajed and Sheffet (2019)
.
As a benchmark, we also compare to the non-private TTUCB.
As for
\epsilon
-global DP, we set the risk
\delta=10^{-2}
, implement all the algorithms in Python (version
3.8
) and run each algorithm
1000
times.
We plot the corresponding average and standard deviations of the empirical stopping times in Figure
1
.
We also test the algorithms on other Bernoulli instances and report the results in Appendix
I
.

Figure 1
:
Empirical stopping time
\tau_{\delta}
(mean
\pm
std. over 1000 runs,
\delta=10^{-2}
) with respect to the privacy budget
\epsilon
for
\epsilon
-local DP on Bernoulli instance
\mu_{1}
(left) and
\mu_{2}
(right). The shaded vertical line separates the two privacy regimes.

Figure
1
shows that
\mathsf{CTB\text{-}TT}
performance has two regimes.
In the low privacy regime (
\epsilon>4
for
\mu_{1}
and
\epsilon>2
for
\mu_{2}
), the
CTB
(\epsilon)
estimator reduces to the
MLE
, and
\mathsf{CTB\text{-}TT}
matches exactly the performance of the non-private TTUCB.
In the high privacy regime (
\epsilon<4
for
\mu_{1}
and
\epsilon<2
for
\mu_{2}
), the price of privacy on the stopping time is a multiplicative
\epsilon^{-2}
.
Therefore, the sample complexity is prohibitively large to be computed numerically for
\epsilon<0.1
.
The switching value of
\epsilon
between the low and high privacy regimes is an order of magnitude higher for
\epsilon
-local DP compared to the one for
\epsilon
-global DP.
This is predictable since local DP provides a “stronger” privacy guarantee at the cost of worse performance.

5.2
Global DP

We compare the performances of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
,
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
and DP-SE for FC-BAI in different Bernoulli instances as in
Sajed and Sheffet (2019)
.
The first instance has means
\mu_{1}=(0.95,0.9,0.9,0.9,0.5)
and the second instance has means
\mu_{2}=(0.75,0.7,0.7,0.7,0.7)
.
As a benchmark, we also compare to the non-private TTUCB. We set the risk
\delta=10^{-2}
and implement all the algorithms in Python (version
3.8
).
We run each algorithm
1000
times, and plot corresponding average and standard deviations of the empirical stopping times in Figure
2
.
We also test the algorithms on other Bernoulli instances and report the results in Appendix
I
.

Figure 2
:
Empirical stopping time
\tau_{\delta}
(mean
\pm
std. over 1000 runs) with respect to the privacy budget
\epsilon
for
\epsilon
-global DP on Bernoulli instance
\mu_{1}
(left) and
\mu_{2}
(right). The shaded vertical line separates the two privacy regimes.

Figure
2
shows that: (a)
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
require fewer samples than DP-SE to provide a
\delta
-correct answer, for different values of
\epsilon
and in all the instances tested.
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
have the same performance in the low privacy regimes, while
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
improves the sample complexity in the high privacy regime, as predicted theoretically. (b) The experimental performance of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
demonstrate two regimes. A high-privacy regime (for
\epsilon<0.1
for
\mu_{1}
and
\epsilon<0.4
for
\mu_{2}
), where the stopping time depends on the privacy budget
\epsilon
, and a low privacy regime (for
\epsilon>0.1
for
\mu_{1}
and
\epsilon>0.4
for
\mu_{2}
), where the performance of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
does not depend on
\epsilon
, and is four times the samples required by TTUCB in the worst case, as shown theoretically.

Conclusion and Perspectives

Table 1
:
Instances of the
TTUCB
meta-algorithm
defined in Algorithm
1
.

We study FC-BAI with
\epsilon
-local DP and
\epsilon
-global DP.
In both settings, we derive a lower bound on the expected sample complexity which quantifies the additional samples needed by a
\delta
-correct BAI strategy to ensure DP.
The lower bounds further suggest the existence of two privacy regimes.
In the
low-privacy regime
, no additional samples are needed, and
privacy can be achieved for free
.
For the
high-privacy regime
, the lower bound reduces to
\Omega({\epsilon}^{-2}T^{\star}_{\text{TV}^{2}}(\nu))
for
\epsilon
-local DP, and to
\Omega({\epsilon}^{-1}T^{\star}_{\text{TV}}(\nu))
for
\epsilon
-global DP.
To match those lower bounds up to multiplicative constants, we propose
\epsilon
-local DP and
\epsilon
-global DP variants of a Top Two algorithm.
For
\epsilon
-local DP, the
\mathsf{CTB\text{-}TT}
algorithm reaches asymptotic optimality by plugging in a private estimator of the means based on Randomised Response.
For
\epsilon
-global DP, our private estimator of the mean runs in
arm-dependent adaptive episodes
and adds
Laplace noise
to ensure a good privacy-utility trade-off.
By solely plugging in this estimator, the AdaP-TT algorithm fails to recover the asymptotic lower bound for instances with highly different mean gaps.
The AdaP-TT
⋆
algorithm overcomes this limitation by adapting the transportation costs
, hence reaching the asymptotic lower bound in the asymptotic high-privacy regime (up to a small multiplicative constant).

The upper bound matches the lower bound by a multiplicative constant in the high privacy regime, and is also loose in some instances in the low privacy regime, due to the mismatch between the KL divergence of Bernoulli distributions and that of Gaussian.
One possible direction to solve this issue is to use transportation costs tailored to Bernoulli for both the Top Two Sampling and the stopping.
Since our bounds only give a clear picture in the high and low privacy regimes, it would be interesting to provide better insights for the regime in-between where both the
\delta
-correctness and the DP constraints are of the same order.
An interesting direction would be to extend the proposed technique to other variants of pure DP, namely
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP and Rényi-DP
(
Mironov, 2017
)
, or other trust models, e.g. shuffle DP
(
Cheu, 2021
;
Girgis et al., 2021
)
.

We thank Emilie Kaufmann and Aurélien Garivier for the interesting conversations.
We also thank Philippe Preux for his support.

Outline

The appendices are organised as follows:

•

B
.

•

C
.

•

, we detail the proofs of our lower bounds for
\epsilon
-local DP (Theorem
10
and Corollary
11
) and
\epsilon
-global DP (Theorem
17
and Corollary
18
).

•

In Appendix
E
, we show that
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
are
\epsilon
-global DP since they use the
DAF
(\epsilon)
estimator of the means.

•

In Appendix
F
, we prove that the
\epsilon
-global DP GLR stopping rules yields
\delta
-correctness regardless of the sampling rule, both when using non-private transportation costs (Lemma
21
) and adapted transportation costs (Lemma
23
).

•

In Appendix

, we detail the proofs of the asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample complexity of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
(Theorem

) and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
(Theorem
24
).

•

In Appendix

, we discuss in more details the number of rounds of adaptivity.

•

Extended experiments are presented in Appendix
I
.

Algorithm 6

TTUCB Algorithm
(
Jourdan and Degenne, 2024
)

1:

Input:
setting parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, algorithmic hyperparameters
(\beta,s,\alpha)\in(0,1)
, e.g.,
(\beta,s,\alpha)=(1/2,1.2,1.2)
, stopping threshold
(c_{a,b}^{G})_{(a,b)\in[K]^{2}}
as in Eq. (
4
), confidence bonuses
(b_{a}^{G})_{a\in[K]}
and transportation costs
(W_{a,b}^{G})_{(a,b)\in[K]^{2}}
as in Eq. (
3
).

2:

Initialisation:
Observe
r_{a}\sim\nu_{a}
for all
a\in[K]
; Initialise the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{n,a}=r_{a}
,
N_{n,a}=1
,
L_{n,a}=N_{n,a}^{a}=0
where
n=K+1
;

3:

for

n>K

do

4:

Set arm
\hat{a}_{n}=\argmax_{a\in[K]}\hat{\mu}_{n,a}
;
\triangleright

Recommendation rule

5:

if

W_{\hat{a}_{n},a}^{G}(\hat{\mu}_{n},N_{n})\geq c_{\hat{a}_{n},a}^{G}(N_{n},\delta)
for all
a\neq\hat{a}_{n}

then

\triangleright

GLR stopping rule

6:

Set
a_{n}=\top
and
return

\hat{a}_{n}
;

7:

end

if

8:

Set arm
B_{n}=\argmax_{a\in[K]}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{n,a}+b_{a}^{G}(N_{n})\right\}
;
\triangleright

UCB leader

9:

Set arm
C_{n}=\argmin_{a\neq B_{n}}W_{B_{n},a}^{G}(\hat{\mu}_{n},N_{n})
;
\triangleright

TC challenger

10:

Set arm
a_{n}=B_{n}
if
N_{n,B_{n}}^{B_{n}}\leq\beta L_{n+1,B_{n}}
, and
a_{n}=C_{n}
otherwise;
\triangleright

\beta
-tracking

11:

Pull
a_{n}
and observe
r_{n}\sim\nu_{a_{n}}
;
\triangleright

Sampling rule

12:

Set
N_{n+1,a_{n}}\leftarrow N_{n,a_{n}}+1
,
L_{n+1,B_{n}}\leftarrow L_{n,B_{n}}+1
,
N^{B_{n}}_{n+1,B_{n}}\leftarrow N^{B_{n}}_{n,B_{n}}+\mathds{1}\left(B_{n}=a_{n}\right)
;

13:

Set
\hat{\mu}_{n+1,a}=N_{n+1,a}^{-1}\sum_{t\in[n]}r_{t}\mathds{1}\left\{a_{t}=a\right\}
and
n\leftarrow n+1
;
\triangleright

Update rule

14:

end

for

Algorithm 9

Algorithm

1:

.

for all

; Initialise the estimators

,

,

\triangleright

Per-arm doubling update grid

for arm
a

Set

and

;

;
\triangleright

Forgetting past observations

8:

Set
\tilde{\mu}_{k_{n,a},a}=\hat{\mu}_{k_{n,a},a}+Y_{k_{n,a},a}
where
Y_{k_{n,a},a}\sim\text{Lap}((\epsilon\tilde{N}_{k_{n,a},a})^{-1})

\triangleright

Private estimator

9:

end

if

\tilde{a}_{n}=\argmax_{a\in[K]}\tilde{\mu}_{n,a}
;
\triangleright

if

Appendix D
Lower Bounds on the Expected Sample Complexity

In this section, we provide the proofs for the sample complexity lower bounds. First, we present the canonical model for BAI to introduce the relevant quantities. Then, we prove the
\epsilon
-local DP sample complexity lower bound. Finally, for global-DP, we first prove an
\epsilon
-global version of the transportation lemma, i.e. Lemma
16
. Using this lemma, we prove the
\epsilon
-global DP sample complexity lower bound of Theorem
17
. We also prove the formula expressing the TV characteristic time for Bernoulli instances.

D.1
Canonical Model for BAI

Let
\bm{\nu}\triangleq\{\nu_{a}:a\in[K]\}
be a bandit instance, consisting of
K
arms with finite means
\{\mu_{a}\}_{a\in[K]}
. Now, we recall the interaction between a BAI strategy
\pi
and the bandit instance
\nu
in the Protocol
3
. The BAI strategy
\pi
halts at
\tau
, samples a sequence of actions
\underline{A}^{\tau}
, and recommends the action
\hat{A}
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
be the probability distribution over the triplets
(\tau,\underline{A}^{\tau},\hat{A})
, when the BAI strategy
\pi
interacts with the bandit instance
\nu
.

For a fixed
T>1
, a sequence of actions
\underline{a}^{T}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{T})\in[K]^{T}
and a recommendation
\hat{a}\in[K]
, we define the event
E=\{\tau=T,\underline{A}^{\tau}=\underline{a}^{T},\hat{A}=\hat{a}\}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}(E)=\int_{\underline{r}^{T}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})\in\real^{T}}\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{a_{t}}(r_{t})dr_{t}

where

\displaystyle\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\operatorname{Rec}_{T+1}\left(\widehat{a}\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\mathrm{S}_{T+1}\left(\top\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)

and
\mathcal{H}_{t}=(a_{1},r_{1},\dots,a_{t},r_{t})
.

Remark on the bandit feedback for global DP.

Let
\pi
be an
\epsilon
-DP BAI strategy. Let
T\geq 1
,
\underline{a}^{T}\in[K]^{T}
a sequence sampled actions and
\widehat{a}\in[K]
a recommended actions. This time, let
\underline{r}^{T}=\{r_{1},\dots,r_{T}\}\in\real^{T}
and
\underline{r^{\prime}}^{T}\in\real^{T}
two neighbouring sequence of rewards, i.e.
d_{\text{Ham}}(\underline{r}^{T},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}\left\{r_{t}\neq r^{\prime}_{t}\right\}=1
. Consider the table of rewards
\underline{d}^{T}
consisting of concatenating
\underline{r}^{T}
colon-wise
K
times, i.e.
\underline{d}^{T}_{t,i}=\underline{r}^{T}_{t}
for all
i\in[K]
and all
t\in[T]
. Define
\underline{d^{\prime}}^{T}
similarly with respect to
\underline{r^{\prime}}^{T}
.

In this case, by definition of
\pi
,
\underline{d}^{T}
and
\underline{d^{\prime}}^{T}
, it is direct that

\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})=\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{d}^{T})

and
d_{\text{Ham}}(\underline{d}^{T},\underline{d^{\prime}}^{T})=1
.

Which means that

\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})\leq e^{\epsilon}\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{r^{\prime}}^{T}).

In other words,
if
\pi
is
\epsilon
-pure DP for neighbouring table of rewards
\underline{d}^{T}
, then
\pi
is also
\epsilon
-pure DP for neighbouring sequence of observed rewards
\underline{r}^{T}
.

Remark on the local DP canonical model.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\pi)
be a pair of perturbation mechanism and BAI satisfying
\epsilon
-local DP. Let
\bm{\nu}\triangleq\{\nu_{a}:a\in[K]\}
be a bandit instance. In the local DP interaction protocol, the BAI strategy
\pi
only accesses the noisy rewards from the perturbation mechanism, i.e.
z_{t}\sim\mathcal{M}(r_{t})
, where
r_{t}\sim\nu_{a_{t}}
. Thus, we can define an environment
\bm{\nu}^{\mathcal{M}}\triangleq\{\nu_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}:a\in[K]\}

induced
by the perturbation mechanism, where

\displaystyle\nu_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(Z)=\int_{r\in\real}\mathcal{M}(Z\mid r)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{a}(r)dr

is the marginal over the noisy rewards of arm
a
.

Thus, the local DP canonical model of the interaction between
(\mathcal{M},\pi)
and an environment
\bm{\nu}
is equivalent to the “classical” canonical model between
\pi
and the induced environment
\bm{\nu}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

D.2
Expected Sample Complexity Lower Bound under
\epsilon
-local DP

Theorem
10
1

(Sample complexity lower bound for
\epsilon
-local DP FC-BAI)

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon>0
.
For any
\delta
-correct
\epsilon
-local DP pair
(\mathcal{M},\pi)
of perturbation mechanism and BAI strategy, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\nu}}[\tau_{\delta}]\geq T^{\star}_{\ell}\left(\bm{\nu};\epsilon\right)\log(1/(2.4\delta))
with

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\ell}\left(\bm{\nu};\epsilon\right)^{-1}\mathrel{\triangleq}\sup\limits_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\inf\limits_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in[K]}\omega_{a}\min\left\{\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),c(\epsilon)\left(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\right)^{2}\right\}\>,

where
c(\epsilon)=\min\{4,e^{2\epsilon}\}\left(e^{\epsilon}-1\right)^{2}
is a privacy term.
For two probability distributions
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
on the measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
, the TV divergence is
\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathbb{P}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{Q}\right)\triangleq\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}}\{\mathbb{P}(A)-\mathbb{Q}(A)\}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\pi)
a perturbation mechanism and BAI strategy pair that it
\epsilon
-local DP.

We suppose that
\pi
is
\delta
-correct.

Using the remark in the local DP canonical model,
\pi
is
\delta
-correct with respect to the environment
\bm{\nu}^{\mathcal{M}}\triangleq\{\nu_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}:a\in[K]\}
induced by the perturbation mechanism
\mathcal{M}
, where

\displaystyle\nu_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(Z)=\int_{r\in\real}\mathcal{M}(Z\mid r)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{a}(r)dr

is the marginal over the noisy rewards of arm
a
.

Thus using Lemma 1 in
Kaufmann et al. (2016)
, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\right)\geq\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta)

for any alternative environment
\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
.

Using Theorem 1 in
Duchi et al. (2013)
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\right)+\mathrm{KL}\left(\lambda^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \nu^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min\{4,e^{2\epsilon}\}(e^{\epsilon}-1)^{2}(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right))^{2}

\displaystyle=c(\epsilon)(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right))^{2}

where
c(\epsilon)\mathrel{\triangleq}\min\{4,e^{2\epsilon}\}(e^{\epsilon}-1)^{2}
.

On the other hand, using the data-processing inequality, we also have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda^{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\right)\leq\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)

Thus, combining the two inequalities gives that

\displaystyle\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\min\left\{\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),c(\epsilon)(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right))^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\sum_{a=1}^{K}\frac{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]}{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]}\min\left\{\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),c(\epsilon)(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right))^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]\left(\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\sum_{a=1}^{K}\omega_{a}\min\left\{\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),c(\epsilon)(\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right))^{2}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau](T^{\star}_{\ell}\left(\bm{\nu},\epsilon\right))^{-1}

The theorem follows by noting that for
\delta\in(0,1),\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta)\geq\log(1/2.4\delta)
.
∎

D.3
Expected Sample Complexity Lower Bound under
\epsilon
-global DP

D.3.1
Upper Bound on the KL as a Transport Problem

First, we recall the result that conditioning increases the KL.

Theorem 26

(Conditioning Increases the KL)
.

Let
P_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{Y\mid X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{Y}
and
P_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{Y\mid X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{Y}
.
Then

\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{Y}\|Q_{Y}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{X}}\left[\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{Y\mid X}\|Q_{Y\mid X}\right)\right].

We apply this result to our problem of upper-bounding the marginals of DP mechanisms.

Theorem
14
2

(KL Upper Bound as a Transport Problem)

If
\mathcal{M}
is
\epsilon
-pure DP,

\mathrm{KL}\left(M_{1}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ M_{2}\right)\leq\epsilon\inf_{\mathcal{C}\in\Pi(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{(D,D^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{C}}[d_{\text{Ham}}(D,D^{\prime})]\>

where
\Pi(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})
is the set of all couplings between
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\Pi(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})
a coupling between
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Then, we re-write:

\displaystyle M_{1}(A)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\int_{D\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\mathcal{M}_{D}\left(A\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(D\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{D,D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\mathcal{M}_{D}\left(A\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{C}\left(D,D^{\prime}\right)

and

\displaystyle M_{2}(A)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\int_{D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\mathcal{M}_{D^{\prime}}\left(A\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{P}_{2}\left(D^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{D,D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\mathcal{M}_{D^{\prime}}\left(A\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{C}\left(D,D^{\prime}\right)

by the definition of the coupling.

Then, using Theorem
26
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(M_{1}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ M_{2}\right)\leq\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{(D,D^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{C}}\left[\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathcal{M}_{D}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{D^{\prime}}\right)\right]

Finally, using group privacy, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathcal{M}_{D}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{D^{\prime}}\right)\leq\epsilon d_{\text{Ham}}(D,D^{\prime})

Combining the last two inequalities gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(M_{1}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ M_{2}\right)\leq\epsilon\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{(D,D^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{C}}[d_{\text{Ham}}(D,D^{\prime})]

And since this is true for any coupling
\mathcal{C}
, taking the infimum over couplings concludes the proof.
∎

D.3.2
Sequential KL Decomposition for Bandits under DP

Now, we adapt Theorem
15
for the bandit marginals. First, for simplicity, we start with the setting where
T
the number of rounds in the bandit interaction is fixed.

Let
\nu=\{P_{a},a\in[K]\}
and
\nu^{\prime}=\{P^{\prime}_{a},a\in[K]\}
be two bandit instances. We recall that, when the policy
\pi
interacts with the bandit instance
\nu
, it induces a marginal distribution
\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}
over the sequence of actions, where

m_{\nu\pi}(a_{1},\dots,a_{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\int_{r_{1},\dots,r_{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid H_{t-1})p_{a_{t}}(r_{t})\,\mathrm{d}r_{t}.

and for all
C\in\mathcal{P}([K]^{T})
,

\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}(C)\mathrel{\triangleq}\sum_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{T})\in C}m_{\nu\pi}(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{T}).

We define
\mathbb{M}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}
similarly.

The goal is to upper bound the quantity
\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}\right)
. The marginals
\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}
in the sequential setting ”look like” marginals generated by ”product distributions”. However, the hardness of the sequential setting resides in the fact that the data-generating distributions depend on the actions chosen, which are stochastic. Thus, the results of the previous section cannot directly be applied. To adapt the proof ideas of the previous section to the bandit case, we introduce the idea of a coupled bandit instance.

Let
\nu=\{P_{a}:a\in[K]\}
and
\nu^{\prime}=\{P^{\prime}_{a}:a\in[K]\}
be two bandit instances. Define
c_{a}
as the maximal coupling between
P_{a}
and
P^{\prime}_{a}
. Fix a policy
\pi=\{\pi_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

Here, we build a coupled environment
\gamma
of
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
. The policy
\pi
interacts with the coupled environment
\gamma
up to a given time horizon
T
to produce an augmented history
\{(a_{t},r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
. The iterative steps of this interaction process are:

1.

The probability of choosing an action
a_{t}=a
at time
t
is dictated only by the policy
\pi_{t}
and
a_{1},r_{1},a_{2},r_{2},\dots,a_{t-1},r_{t-1}
,
i.e.
the policy ignores
\{r^{\prime}_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}
.

2.

The distribution of rewards
(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})
is
c_{a_{t}}
and is conditionally independent of the previous observed history
\{(a_{s},r_{s},r^{\prime}_{s})\}_{t=1}^{t-1}
.

This interaction is similar to the interaction process of policy
\pi
with the first bandit instance
\nu
, with the addition of sampling an extra
r^{\prime}_{t}
from the coupling of
P_{a_{t}}
and
P^{\prime}_{a_{t}}
. This, in essence, corresponds to the ”up” branch in Theorem
26
.

The distribution of the augmented history induced by the interaction of
\pi
and the coupled environment can be defined as

\displaystyle p_{\gamma\pi}(a_{1},r_{1},r^{\prime}_{1}\dots,a_{T},r_{T},r^{\prime}_{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid a_{1},r_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},r_{t-1})c_{a_{t}}(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})

To simplify the notation, let
\textbf{a}\mathrel{\triangleq}(a_{1},\dots,a_{T})
,
\textbf{r}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})
and
\textbf{r'}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{T})
. Also, let
c_{\textbf{a}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\mathrel{\triangleq}\prod_{t=1}^{T}c_{a_{t}}(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})
and
\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r})\mathrel{\triangleq}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid a_{1},r_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},r_{t-1})
. We put
\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})
.

With the new notation

p_{\gamma\pi}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\mathrel{\triangleq}\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r})c_{\textbf{a}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})

Similarly, we define

q_{\gamma\pi}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\mathrel{\triangleq}\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r'})c_{\textbf{a}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})

which corresponds to the ”down” branch in Theorem
26
, where the policy ignores the rewards
r_{1},\dots,r_{T}
in the interaction.

It follows that
m_{\nu\pi}
is the marginal of
p_{\gamma\pi}
when integrated over
(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})
, and
m_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}
is the marginal of
q_{\gamma\pi}
when integrated over
(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})
,
i.e.

m_{\nu\pi}(\textbf{a})=\int_{\textbf{r},\textbf{r'}}p_{\gamma\pi}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\,\mathrm{d}\textbf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\textbf{r'}

and

m_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}(\textbf{a})=\int_{\textbf{r},\textbf{r'}}q_{\gamma\pi}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\,\mathrm{d}\textbf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\textbf{r'}.

By the data-processing inequality, we get that

\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}\right)\leq\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{\gamma\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ q_{\gamma\pi}\right)

We have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{\gamma\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ q_{\gamma\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r})c_{\textbf{a}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})}{\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r'})c_{\textbf{a}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r}}{\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r'}}\right)\right]

where:
(a): by definition of
p_{\gamma\pi}
,
q_{\gamma\pi}
and the KL divergence

Global DP.
Now, if the policy
\pi
is
\epsilon
-global DP, then by group privacy
\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r})\leq e^{\epsilon d_{\text{Ham}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})}\pi(\textbf{a}\mid\textbf{r'})
for any sequence of actions, and any two sequence of rewards
r
and
r’
. Thus, computing the expectation of
d_{\text{Ham}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})
when
r
and
r’
are generated through the coupled environment provides the following theorem.

Theorem 27

(KL Decomposition for
\epsilon
-global DP)
.

If
\pi
is
\epsilon
-global DP, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\nu\pi}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}t_{a_{t}}\right),

where
t_{a_{t}}\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathrm{TV}\left(P_{a_{t}}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a_{t}}\right)
and
\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\nu\pi}
is the expectation under
m_{\nu\pi}
.

Proof.

The proof follows by computing

\displaystyle\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}^{\pi}_{\textbf{r}}(\textbf{a})}{\mathcal{V}_{\textbf{r'}}(\textbf{a})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\epsilon d_{\text{Ham}}(\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\mathds{1}\left\{r_{t}\neq r^{\prime}_{t}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\mathds{1}\left\{r_{t}\neq r^{\prime}_{t}\right\}|a_{t}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\textbf{a},\textbf{r},\textbf{r'})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[t_{a_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{a}\sim m_{\nu\pi}}\left[t_{a_{t}}\right]

where:
(a) is by group privacy, (b) is by the definition of the hamming distance, (c) is by the towering property of the expectation, (d) is by the definition of the maximal coupling and (e) is because the sum only depends on the sequence of actions, with marginal distribution
m_{\nu\pi}
.
∎

Comparison to the product distribution setting:
The result of Theorem
27
generalises the result of Theorem
15
to the sequential setting under pure DP. Since the actions are stochastic, there is an additional expectation over the generation process of the sequence of actions, sampled from
\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}
. Also, Theorem
27
can be seen as an
\epsilon
-DP version of the general KL decomposition lemma (Exercice 15.8, (b) in
Lattimore and Szepesvári (2020)
), which recall states that
\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{P}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}\right)=\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\nu\pi}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{a_{t}}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a_{t}}\right)\right)
.

Remark 28

(Improvement of a factor of
6
for
Azize and Basu 2022
)
.

Theorem

of
Azize and Basu (2022)
states

\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\nu^{\prime}\pi}\right)\leq 6\epsilon\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\nu\pi}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}t_{a_{t}}\right).

We used a generalisation of Karwa Vadhan lemma to prove this result for product distributions. Using the coupled environment idea in the new proof, we eliminate the extra
6
factor in the upper bound. This improves all the regret and sample complexity lower bounds in this manuscript by a factor of
6
compared to the results in
Azize and Basu (2022)
;
Azize et al. (2023)
.

Remark 29

(Stopping time version of the KL decomposition under DP)
.

Let
\pi

be a DP BAI strategy. Let
\nu
and
\lambda
be two bandit instances. Denote by
\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
the marginal distribution of
(\underline{A},\widehat{A},\tau)
, i.e. the sequence of action, final recommendation and stopping time when the BAI strategy
\pi
interacts with
\nu
. By adapting the proof technique seen before for the canonical bandit setting under FC-BAI, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\lambda\pi}\right)\leq\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(P_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a}\right)

where
\tau
is the stopping time,
t_{a_{t}}\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathrm{TV}\left(P_{a_{t}}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a_{t}}\right)
and
\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\nu\pi}
is the expectation under
m_{\nu\pi}
.

Proof.

For completeness, we recall the canonical bandit setting under FC-BAI, using the notation of Remark
29
, and then adapt the coupling technique to FC-BAI. The main difference with the previous section is that the number of interactions
\tau
is now a random variable, and we use Wald’s lemma to deal with that.

Step 1: Canonical Model under FC-BAI:
Let
\bm{\nu}\triangleq\{P_{a}:a\in[K]\}
be a bandit instance, consisting of
K
arms with finite means
\{\mu_{a}\}_{a\in[K]}
. Now, we recall the interaction between a BAI strategy
\pi
and the bandit instance
\nu
in the Protocol
3
. The BAI strategy
\pi
halts at
\tau
, samples a sequence of actions
\underline{A}^{\tau}\mathrel{\triangleq}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\tau})
, and recommends the action
\hat{A}
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
be the marginal distribution over the output
(\tau,\underline{A}^{\tau},\hat{A})
, when the BAI strategy
\pi
interacts with the bandit instance
\nu
. Then,

\displaystyle m_{\bm{\nu},\pi}(\tau=T,\underline{A}^{\tau}=\underline{a}^{T},\hat{A}=\hat{a})=\int_{\underline{r}^{T}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})\in\real^{T}}\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}p_{a_{t}}(r_{t})dr_{t}

where

\displaystyle\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{r}^{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\operatorname{Rec}_{T+1}\left(\widehat{a}\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\mathrm{S}_{T+1}\left(\top\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)

and
\mathcal{H}_{t}=(a_{1},r_{1},\dots,a_{t},r_{t})
.

Step 2: Coupling technique applied to FC-BAI marginals.
Let
\nu=\{P_{a}:a\in[K]\}
and
\lambda=\{P^{\prime}_{a}:a\in[K]\}
be two bandit instances. Define
c_{a}
as the maximal coupling between
P_{a}
and
P^{\prime}_{a}
. Here, we build again a coupled environment
\gamma
of
\nu
and
\lambda
. The BAI strategy
\pi
interacts with the coupled environment
\gamma
, decides to halt at step
\tau
to produce an augmented history
(\tau,a_{1},r_{1},r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a_{\tau},r_{\tau},r^{\prime}_{\tau},\hat{a})
. The iterative steps of this interaction process are:

1.

The probability of choosing an action
a_{t}=a
at time
t
is dictated only by the sampling rule
S_{t}
and
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\mathrel{\triangleq}(a_{1},r_{1},a_{2},r_{2},\dots,a_{t-1},r_{t-1})
,
i.e.
the sampling rule ignores
\{r^{\prime}_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}
.

2.

The distribution of rewards
(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})
is
c_{a_{t}}
and is conditionally independent of the previous observed history
\{(a_{s},r_{s},r^{\prime}_{s})\}_{t=1}^{t-1}
.

3.

The stopping rule and recommendation rule only depend on the history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, the stopping and recommendation rules ignore
\{r^{\prime}_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}

The distribution of the augmented history induced by the interaction of
\pi
and the coupled environment can be defined as

\displaystyle p_{\gamma\pi}(\tau,a_{1},r_{1},r^{\prime}_{1}\dots,a_{\tau},r_{\tau},r^{\prime}_{\tau},\hat{a})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\operatorname{Rec}_{\tau+1}\left(\widehat{a}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\tau}\right)\mathrm{S}_{\tau+1}\left(\top\mid\mathcal{H}_{\tau}\right)\prod_{t=1}^{\tau}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)c_{a_{t}}(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})

\displaystyle=\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r}^{\tau})\prod_{t=1}^{\tau}c_{a_{t}}(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})

where
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{t}\in[K]
and
r_{t}\in\real
are fixed.

To simplify the notation, let
\underline{r}^{\tau}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r_{1},\dots,r_{\tau})
,
\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{\tau})
and
c_{\underline{a}^{\tau}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})\mathrel{\triangleq}\prod_{t=1}^{\tau}c_{a_{t}}(r_{t},r^{\prime}_{t})
. We put
\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})
.

With the new notation

p_{\gamma\pi}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\mathrel{\triangleq}\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r}^{\tau})c_{\underline{a}^{\tau}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})

Similarly, we define

q_{\gamma\pi}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\mathrel{\triangleq}\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})c_{\underline{a}^{\tau}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})

which corresponds to the augmented history interaction, where the policy ignores the rewards
(r_{1},\dots,r_{t},\dots)
in the interaction.

It follows that
m_{\nu\pi}
and
m_{\lambda\pi}
are the marginals of
p_{\gamma\pi}
and
q_{\gamma\pi}
when integrated over the rewards,
i.e.

m_{\nu\pi}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a})=\int_{\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau}}p_{\gamma\pi}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})\prod_{t=1}^{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}r_{t}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}_{t}

and

m_{\lambda\pi}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a})=\int_{\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau}}q_{\gamma\pi}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})\prod_{t=1}^{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}r_{t}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}_{t}

By the data-processing inequality, we get that

\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\lambda\pi}\right)\leq\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{\gamma\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ q_{\gamma\pi}\right)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{\gamma\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ q_{\gamma\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r}^{\tau})c_{\underline{a}^{\tau}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})}{\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})c_{\underline{a}^{\tau}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r}^{\tau})}{\pi(\underline{a}^{\tau},\hat{a},\tau\mid\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\epsilon d_{\text{Ham}}(\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\epsilon\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\mathds{1}\left\{r_{t}\neq r^{\prime}_{t}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\epsilon\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}\left[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{a}(\tau)}\mathds{1}\left\{r_{s,a}\neq r^{\prime}_{s,a}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\textbf{h}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\tau,\underline{a}^{\tau},\underline{r}^{\tau},\underline{r^{\prime}}^{\tau},\hat{a})\sim p_{\gamma\pi}}[N_{a}(\tau)]\;\mathrm{TV}\left(P_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(P_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{a}\right)

where:
(a): by definition of
p_{\gamma\pi}
,
q_{\gamma\pi}
and the KL divergence
(b) is by group privacy, (c) is by the definition of the hamming distance, (d) is using Wald’s lemma and the definition of the maximal coupling, (e) is because
N_{a}(\tau)
does not depend on the sequence of rewards
(r^{\prime}_{t})
, when
h
is generated through
p_{\gamma,\pi}
.

∎

D.3.3
Transportation Lemma under
\epsilon
-global DP: Proof of Lemma
16

Lemma
16
3

(Transportation lemma under
\epsilon
-global DP)

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon>0
. Let
\bm{\nu}
be a bandit instance and
\lambda\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
. For any
\delta
-correct
\epsilon
-global DP BAI strategy, we have that

\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\geq\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta),

where
\mathrm{kl}(x,y)\mathrel{\triangleq}x\log\frac{x}{y}+(1-x)\log\frac{1-x}{1-y}\;
for
x,y\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

Step 1: Distinguishability due to
\delta
-correctness.
Let
\pi
be a
\delta
-correct
\epsilon
-global DP BAI strategy. Let
\bm{\nu}
be a bandit instance and
\lambda\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
denote the probability distribution of
(\underline{A},\widehat{A},\tau)
when the BAI strategy
\pi
interacts with
\nu
. For any alternative instance
\lambda\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
, the data-processing inequality gives that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\bm{\lambda},\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{kl}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left(\widehat{A}=a^{\star}(\bm{\nu})\right),\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\lambda},\pi}\left(\widehat{A}=a^{\star}(\bm{\nu})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta).

(16)

where the second inequality is because
\pi
is
\delta
-correct i.e.
\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left(\widehat{A}=a^{\star}(\bm{\nu})\right)\geq 1-\delta
and
\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\lambda},\pi}\left(\widehat{A}=a^{\star}(\bm{\nu})\right)\leq\delta
, and the monotonicity of the
\mathrm{kl}
.

Step 2: Using the KL decomposition under global DP.
By Remark
29
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \mathbb{M}_{\bm{\lambda},\pi}\right)\leq\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\bm{\nu},\pi}\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right).

(17)

Combining Inequalities
16
and
17
concludes the proof.
∎

D.3.4
Proof of Theorem
17

Theorem
17
4

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon>0
.
For any
\delta
-correct and
\epsilon
-global DP FC-BAI algorithm, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\nu}}[\tau_{\delta}]\geq T^{\star}_{g}\left(\bm{\nu};\epsilon\right)\log(1/(2.4\delta))
with

T^{\star}_{g}\left(\bm{\nu};\epsilon\right)^{-1}\mathrel{\triangleq}\sup\limits_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\inf\limits_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\min\bigg\{\sum_{a\in[K]}\omega_{a}\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),\epsilon\sum_{a\in[K]}\omega_{a}\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\bigg\}\>.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be a
\delta
-correct
\epsilon
-global DP BAI strategy. Let
\bm{\nu}
be a bandit instance and
\lambda\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
.

Let
\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits
denote the expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
, ie
\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits_{\bm{\nu},\pi}
.

By Lemma
16
, we have that

\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\geq\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta).

Lemma 1 from
Kaufmann et al. (2016)
gives that
\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\geq\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta).

Since these two inequalities hold for all
\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{kl}(1-

\displaystyle\delta,\delta)\leq\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\min\left(\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\min\left(\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\frac{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]}{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]}\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),\sum_{a=1}^{K}\frac{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]}{\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]}\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]\left(\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\min\left(\epsilon\sum_{a=1}^{K}\omega_{a}\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right),\sum_{a=1}^{K}\omega_{a}\mathrm{KL}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)\right)\right)\>.

(a) is due to the fact that
\mathop{\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}}\nolimits[\tau]
does not depend on
\bm{\lambda}
. (b) is obtained by noting that the vector
\left(\omega_{a}\right)_{a\in[K]}\triangleq\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi}\left[N_{a}(\tau)\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi}[\tau]}\right)_{a\in[K]}
belongs to the simplex
\Sigma_{K}
.

The theorem follows by noting that for
\delta\in(0,1),\mathrm{kl}(1-\delta,\delta)\geq\log(1/(2.4\delta))
.
∎

D.3.5
TV Characteristic Time for Bernoulli Instances: Proof of Corollary
18

Proposition 30

(TV characteristic time for Bernoulli instances)
.

Let
\nu
be a bandit instance, i.e. such that
\nu_{a}=\text{Bernoulli}(\mu_{a})
and
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{K}
. Let
\Delta_{a}\mathrel{\triangleq}\mu_{1}-\mu_{a}
and
\Delta_{\text{min}}\mathrel{\triangleq}\min_{a\neq 1}\Delta_{a}
. We have that

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})=\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}\leq T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})\leq\frac{K}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}.

Proof.

Step 1:
Let
\nu
be a bandit instance, i.e. such that
\nu_{a}\mathrel{\triangleq}\text{Bernoulli}(\mu_{a})
and
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{K}
.

For the alternative bandit instance
\bm{\lambda}
, we refer to the mean of arm
a
as
\rho_{a}
, i.e.
\lambda_{a}\mathrel{\triangleq}\text{Bernoulli}(\rho_{a})
.

By the definition of
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left(T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})}\sum_{a=1}^{K}\omega_{a}\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\min_{a\neq 1}\inf_{\bm{\lambda}:\rho_{a}>\rho_{1}}\omega_{1}\left|\mu_{1}-\rho_{1}\right|+\omega_{a}\left|\mu_{a}-\rho_{a}\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\min_{a\neq 1}\min(\omega_{1},\omega_{a})\Delta_{a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sup_{\omega\in\Sigma_{K}}\omega_{1}\min_{a\neq 1}\min(1,\frac{\omega_{a}}{\omega_{1}})\Delta_{a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\sup_{(x_{2},\dots,x_{K})\in(\real^{+})^{K-1}}\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})}{1+x_{2}+\dots+x_{K}}\>,

where
g_{a}(x_{a})\mathrel{\triangleq}\min(1,x_{a})\Delta_{a}
.

Equality (a) is obtained due to the fact that
\operatorname{Alt}(\bm{\nu})=\bigcup_{a\neq 1}\{\bm{\lambda}:\rho_{a}>\rho_{1}\}
, and for Bernoullis,
\mathrm{TV}\left(\nu_{a}\penalty\ \middle\|\penalty\ \lambda_{a}\right)=|\mu_{a}-\rho_{a}|
.

Equality (b) is true, since
\inf_{\bm{\lambda}:\rho_{a}>\rho_{1}}\omega_{1}\left|\mu_{1}-\rho_{1}\right|+\omega_{a}\left|\mu_{a}-\rho_{a}\right|=\min(\omega_{1},\omega_{a})\Delta_{a}
.

Equality (c) holds true, since
\omega_{1}\neq 1
(if
\omega_{1}=0
, the value of the objective is 0).

Equality (d) is obtained by the change of variable
x_{a}\mathrel{\triangleq}\frac{\omega_{a}}{\omega_{1}}

Step 2:
Let
(x_{2},\dots,x_{K})\in(\real^{+})^{K-1}
. By the definition of
g_{a}
, we have that

g_{a}(x_{a})\leq x_{a}\Delta_{a}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{a}(x_{a})\leq\Delta_{a}.

This leads to the inequalities

\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})\leq g_{a}(x_{a})\leq x_{a}\Delta_{a}\quad\text{and}\quad\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})\leq\Delta_{\text{min}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\left(\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})\right)\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})}{\Delta_{a}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{a=2}^{K}x_{a}\>.

This means that for every
(x_{2},\dots,x_{K})\in(\real^{+})^{K-1}
,

\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}g_{a}(x_{a})}{1+x_{2}+\dots+x_{K}}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}}.

Here, the upper bound is achievable for
x^{\star}_{a}=\frac{\Delta_{\text{min}}}{\Delta_{a}}
, since
g_{a}(x^{\star}_{a})=\Delta_{\text{min}}
for all
a\neq 1
.

This concludes that

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}}

\displaystyle\implies T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})=\frac{1}{\Delta_{\text{min}}}+\sum_{a=2}^{K}\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}\>.

Step 3:
The lower and upper bounds on
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})
follow from the fact that
\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}\geq 0
for all
a
, and
\frac{1}{\Delta_{a}}\leq\frac{1}{\Delta_{\min}}
for all
a\neq 1
.

Hence, we conclude the proof.
∎

D.3.6
On the Total Variation Distance and the Hardness of Privacy

Our lower bound suggests that the hardness of the DP-FC-BAI problem is characterized by
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})
, which is a total variation counterpart of the classic KL-based characteristic time
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu})
in FC-BAI
Garivier and Kaufmann (2016)
. The total variation distance appears to be the natural measure to quantify the hardness of privacy in other settings such as regret minimization
Azize and Basu (2022)
, Karwa-Vadhan lemma
Karwa and Vadhan (2018)
and Differentially Private Assouad, Fano, and Le Cam
Acharya et al. (2021)
. The high-level intuition is that: Pure DP can be seen as a multiplicative stability constraint of
e^{\epsilon}
when one data point changes. With group privacy, if two data sets differ in
d_{ham}
points, then one incurs a factor
e^{d_{ham}\penalty\ \epsilon}
. Now, by sampling
n
i.i.d points from a distribution
P
and
n
i.i.d points from a distribution
Q
, the Karwa-Vadhan lemma states that the incurred factor is
e^{(nTV(P,Q))\penalty\ \epsilon}
. This is proved by building a maximal coupling, which is the coupling that minimizes the Hamming distance in expectation. In brief,
the total variation naturally appears in lower bounds since it is the quantity that characterises the hardness of the optimal transport problem minimizing the hamming distance
, i.e
\mathrm{TV}(P,Q)=\inf_{(X,Y)\sim(P,Q)}E(1_{X\neq Y})
. However, it is possible that the problem can be characterized by other f-divergences. Finally, one can always go from TV to KL using Pinsker’s inequality, though that would always be less tight than the TV-based lower bound.

On the relation between
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})
and
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu})
.

A direct application of Pinsker’s inequality gives that
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})\geq\sqrt{2T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu})}
. For completeness, we present here the exact calculations:

For every alternative mean parameter
\lambda
and every arm
a
, using Pinkser’s inequality, we have that
d_{TV}(\mu_{a},\lambda_{a})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}d_{KL}(\mu_{a},\lambda_{a})}
.
Therefore, for every allocation over arms
\omega
, we have

\sum_{a}\omega_{a}d_{TV}(\mu_{a},\lambda_{a})\leq\sum_{a}\omega_{a}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}d_{KL}(\mu_{a},\lambda_{a})}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a}\omega_{a}d_{KL}(\mu_{a},\lambda_{a})}\>.

Taking the supremum over the simplex and the infimum over the set of alternative mean parameters yields
T^{\star}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{\nu})^{-1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}T^{\star}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\bm{\nu})^{-1}}
.
This concludes the proof.

Appendix E
Privacy analysis

We prove that
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
satisfy
\epsilon
-global DP. We first provide the privacy lemma that justifies using doubling and forgetting. Using the privacy lemma and the post-processing property of DP, we conclude the privacy analysis of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
.

E.1
Privacy Lemma for Non-overlapping Sequences

Lemma 31

(Privacy of non-overlapping sequence of empirical means)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a mechanism that takes a
set
as input and outputs the private empirical mean, i.e.,

\mathcal{M}(\{r_{i},\dots,r_{j}\})\mathrel{\triangleq}\frac{1}{j-i}\sum_{t=i}^{j}r_{t}+Lap\left(\frac{1}{(j-i)\epsilon}\right)\>.

Let
\ell<T
and
t_{1},\ldots t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}
be in
[1,T]
such that
1=t_{1}<\cdots<t_{\ell}<t_{\ell+1}-1=T
.

Let’s define the following mechanism

\mathcal{G}:\{r_{1},\dots,r_{T}\}\rightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\{r_{t_{i}},\ldots,r_{t_{i+1}-1}\}}

(18)

In other words,
\mathcal{G}
is the mechanism we get by applying
\mathcal{M}
to the non-overlapping partition of the sequence
\{r_{1},\dots,r_{T}\}
according to
t_{1}<\cdots<t_{\ell}<t_{\ell+1}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}r_{1}\\
r_{2}\\
\vdots\\
r_{T}\end{pmatrix}\overset{\mathcal{G}}{\rightarrow}\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mu_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}

where
\mu_{i}\sim\mathcal{M}_{\{r_{t_{i}},\ldots,r_{t_{i+1}-1}\}}
.

For
r_{t}\in[0,1]
, the mechanism
\mathcal{G}
is
\epsilon
-DP.

Proof.

Let
r^{T}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})
and
r^{\prime T}\mathrel{\triangleq}(r_{1}^{\prime},\dots,r_{T}^{\prime})
be two neighbouring reward sequences in [0,1]. This implies that
\exists j\in[1,T]
such that
r_{j}\neq r_{j}^{\prime}
and
\forall t\neq j
,
r_{t}=r_{t}^{\prime}
.

Let
\ell^{\prime}
be such that
t_{\ell^{\prime}}\leq j\leq t_{\ell^{\prime}+1}-1
, and follows the convention that
t_{0}=1
and
t_{\ell+1}=T+1
.

Let
\mu\mathrel{\triangleq}(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell})
a fixed sequence of outcomes. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}(r^{T})=\mu)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}(r^{\prime T})=\mu)}=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{M}(\{r_{{t_{\ell^{\prime}}}},\dots,r_{t_{\ell^{\prime}+1}-1}\})=\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{M}(\{r_{{t_{\ell^{\prime}}}},\dots,r_{t_{\ell^{\prime}+1}-1}\})=\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}\leq e^{\epsilon},

where the last inequality holds true because
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\epsilon
-DP following Theorem
3
.
∎

E.2
Privacy Analysis of
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}

Theorem 32

(Privacy analysis)
.

For rewards in
[0,1]
,
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
and
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
satisfy
\epsilon
-global DP.

Remark 33
.

The following proof is valid for any BAI strategy that only uses the DAF(
\epsilon
) to estimate the means.

Proof.

Let
T\geq 1
. Let
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}=\{\textbf{x}_{1},\dots,\textbf{x}_{T}\}
and
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}=\{\textbf{d}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\textbf{d}^{\prime}_{T}\}
two neighbouring reward tables in
(\real^{K})^{T}
. Let
j\in[1,T]
such that, for all
t\neq j
,
d_{t}=d^{\prime}_{t}
.

We also fix a sequence of sampled actions
\underline{a}^{T}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{T}\}\in[K]^{T}
and a recommended action
\hat{a}\in K
.

Let
\pi
be a BAI strategy that only uses DAF(
\epsilon
) to estimate the means, i.e. either
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}
or
\mathsf{AdaP\text{-}TT}^{\star}
.

We want to show that:

\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T})\leq e^{\epsilon}\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T})
.

The main idea is that the change of reward in the
j
-th reward only affects the empirical mean computed in one episode, which is made private using the Laplace Mechanism and Lemma
31
.

Step 1. Sequential decomposition of the output probability

We observe that due to the sequential nature of the interaction, the output probability can be decomposed to a part that depends on
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}\mathrel{\triangleq}\{\textbf{x}_{1},\dots,\textbf{x}_{j-1}\}
, which is identical for both
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}
and
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}
and a second conditional part on the history.

Specifically, we have that

\displaystyle\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\operatorname{Rec}_{T+1}\left(\widehat{a}\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\mathrm{S}_{T+1}\left(\top\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}}(\underline{a}^{j})\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})

where

•

a_{>j}\mathrel{\triangleq}(a_{j+1},\dots,a_{T})

•

\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}}(\underline{a}^{j})\mathrel{\triangleq}\prod_{t=1}^{j}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)

•

\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})\mathrel{\triangleq}\operatorname{Rec}_{T+1}\left(\widehat{a}\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\mathrm{S}_{T+1}\left(\top\mid\mathcal{H}_{T}\right)\prod_{t=j+1}^{T}\mathrm{\penalty\ S}_{t}\left(a_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right)

Similarly

\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T})\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}}(\underline{a}^{j})\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})

since
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{j-1}=\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}
.

Which means that

\frac{\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T})}{\pi(\underline{a}^{T},\widehat{a},T\mid\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T})}=\frac{\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})}{\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})}

(19)

Step 2. The adaptive episodes are the same, before step
j

Let
\ell
such that
t_{\ell}\leq j<t_{\ell+1}
when
\pi
interacts with
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}
. Let us call it
\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}}(j)\mathrel{\triangleq}\ell
.

Similarly, let
\ell^{\prime}
such that
t_{\ell^{\prime}}\leq j<t_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
when
\pi
interacts with
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}
. Let us call it
\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}}(j)\mathrel{\triangleq}\ell^{\prime}
.

Since
\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}}(j)
only depends on
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{j-1}
, which is identical for
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}
and
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}
, we have that
\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}}(j)=\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}}(j)
with probability
1
.

We call
\xi_{j}
the last
time-step
of the episode
\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}}(j)
, i.e
\xi_{j}\mathrel{\triangleq}t_{\psi^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}}(j)+1}-1
.

Step 3. Private sufficient statistics

Let
r_{t}\mathrel{\triangleq}\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}_{t,a_{t}}
, be the reward corresponding to the action
a_{t}
in the table
\underline{\textbf{d}}^{T}
. Similarly,
r^{\prime}_{t}\mathrel{\triangleq}\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}_{t,a_{t}}
for
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}^{T}
.

Let us define
L_{j}\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathcal{G}_{\{r_{1},\dots,r_{\xi_{j}}\}}
and
L^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\triangleq}\mathcal{G}_{\{r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{\xi_{j}}\}}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is defined as in Eq.
18
, using the same episodes for
d
and
d^{\prime}
. In other words,
L_{j}
is the list of private empirical means computed on a non-overlapping sequence of rewards before step
\xi_{j}
.

Using the forgetting structure of
\pi
, there exists a randomised mapping
f_{\underline{\textbf{d}}_{>\xi_{j}}}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(.\mid\underline{a}^{j})=f_{\underline{\textbf{d}}_{>\xi_{j}}}\circ L_{j}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}}(.\mid\underline{a}^{j})=f_{\underline{\textbf{d}}_{>\xi_{j}}}\circ L_{j}^{\prime}
.

In other words, the interaction of
\pi
with
\underline{\textbf{d}}
and
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}
from step
\xi_{j}+1
until
T
only depends on the sufficient statistics
L_{j}
, which summarises what happened before
\xi_{j}
, and the new inputs
\underline{\textbf{d}}_{>\xi_{j}}
, which are the same for
\underline{\textbf{d}}
and
\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}
.

Step 4. Concluding with Lemma
31
and the post-processing lemma

Since rewards are in
[0,1]
, using Lemma
31
, we have that
\mathcal{G}
is
\epsilon
-DP.

Since
\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(.\mid\underline{a}^{j})
is just a post-processing of the output of
\mathcal{G}
, we have that

\frac{\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})}{\mathcal{P}^{\pi}_{\underline{\textbf{d}^{\prime}}}(a_{>j},\hat{a},T\mid\underline{a}^{j})}\leq e^{\epsilon}\>,

and Eq. (
19
) concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Globally Differentially Private GLR Stopping Rules

After studying the non-private GLR stopping rule with phases (Appendix
F.1
), we study the private GLR stopping rule with non-private transportation costs
W^{G}_{a,b}
in Appendix
F.2
(Lemma
21
) and with adapted transportation costs
W^{G,\epsilon}_{a,b}
in Appendix
F.3
(Lemma
23
).

F.1
Non-private GLR Stopping Rule with Per-arm Phases

Before accounting for the privacy (i.e. Laplace noise), we first highlight the price of
DAF
(\epsilon)
for
\epsilon=+\infty
.
This stopping condition is only evaluated at the beginning of each phase for each arm since it involves quantities that are fixed until we switch phase again, and it recommends
\hat{a}_{n}=\argmax_{a\in[K]}\hat{\mu}_{k_{n,a},a}
which is the best arm for the non-private empirical means.
Lemma
34
yields a threshold function ensuring
\delta
-correctness.

Lemma 34
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
.
Let
s>1
,
\zeta
be the Riemann
\zeta
function,
c^{G}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
4
) and
k(x)=\log_{2}x+2
.
Combining the
DAF
(\epsilon)
estimator for
\epsilon=+\infty
with the GLR stopping rule with
W^{G}_{a,b}
as in Eq. (
3
) and the stopping threshold
c^{G}_{a,b}(\omega,\delta(\zeta(s)^{2}k(\omega_{a})^{s}k(\omega_{b})^{s})^{-1})
yields a
\delta
-correct algorithm for
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian distributions regardless of the sampling rule.

Proof.

The non-private GLR stopping rule matches the one used for Gaussian bandits.
Proving
\delta
-correctness of a GLR stopping rule is done by leveraging concentration results.

Lemma 35

(Theorem 9 in
Kaufmann and Koolen 2021
)
.

Let
\bm{\nu}
be a sub-Gaussian bandit with means
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and variance proxy
\sigma
. Let
S\subseteq[K]
and
x>0
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\nu}}\left(\exists n\in\mathbb{N},\>\sum_{a\in S}\frac{N_{n,a}}{2\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{n,a}-\mu_{a})^{2}>\sum_{a\in S}2\log\left(4+\log\left(N_{n,a}\right)\right)+|S|\mathcal{C}_{G}\left(\frac{x}{|S|}\right)\right)\leq e^{-x}\>,

where
\mathcal{C}_{G}
is defined in
